**Violence against women** (**VAW**), also known as **gender-based violence**,[^cite_note-1] **violence against women and girls**, or **sexual and gender-based violence**,[^cite_note-2] is [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent) committed against [women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woman) or [girls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girl) specifically because they are [female](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Female). Such violence, often considered [hate crime](13547.md),[^cite_note-3] is primarily committed by [men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man) or [boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boy) due to historical power dynamics between the genders. [Violence against men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence_against_men) is the corresponding category, where acts of violence are targeted against males.

VAW has an extensive history, though the incidents and intensity of violence has varied over time and between societies. Such violence is often seen as a mechanism for the subjugation of women, whether in society in general or in an [interpersonal relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interpersonal_relationship).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrownmiller197515-17-4][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiles19881-14-5]

The UN [Declaration on the Elimination of Violence Against Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_on_the_Elimination_of_Violence_Against_Women) states, "violence against women is a manifestation of historically unequal power relations between men and women" and "violence against women is one of the crucial social mechanisms by which women are forced into a subordinate position compared with men."[^cite_note-6]

[Kofi Annan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofi_Annan), [Secretary-General of the United Nations](162415.md), declared in a 2006 report posted on the [United Nations Development Fund for Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Fund_for_Women) (UNIFEM) website:

> Violence against women and girls is a problem of [pandemic](24255.md) proportions. At least one out of every three women around the world has been beaten, coerced into sex, or otherwise abused in her lifetime with the abuser usually someone known to her.[^cite_note-Moradian-7]

## Definition

International instruments aiming to eliminate violence against women and domestic violence have been enacted by various international bodies. These generally start with a definition of gendered violence, and a proposal to combat it. The Istanbul Convention ([Council of Europe Convention on preventing and combating violence against women and domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe_Convention_on_preventing_and_combating_violence_against_women_and_domestic_violence)) of the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) describes VAW as "a violation of human rights and a form of discrimination against women" and defines VAW as "all acts of gender-based violence that result in or are likely to result in physical, sexual, psychological, or economic harm or suffering to women, including threats of such acts, coercion, or arbitrary deprivation of liberty, whether occurring in public or in private life".[^cite_note-COE_210-8]

The 1979 [Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Discrimination_Against_Women) (CEDAW) of the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) makes recommendations relating to VAW,[^cite_note-9] and the [Vienna Declaration and Programme of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna_Declaration_and_Programme_of_Action) mentions VAW.[^cite_note-10] However, the 1993 United Nations General Assembly resolution on the [Declaration on the Elimination of Violence Against Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_on_the_Elimination_of_Violence_Against_Women) was the first international instrument to explicitly define VAW and elaborate on the subject.[^cite_note-11] Other definitions of VAW are set out in the 1994 *Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment, and Eradication of Violence Against Women*[^cite_note-12] and by the 2003 [Maputo Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maputo_Protocol).[^cite_note-13]

In addition, the term *gender-based violence* refers to "any acts or threats of acts intended to hurt or make women suffer physically, sexually, or psychologically, and which affect women because they are women or affect women disproportionately".[^cite_note-14] *Gender-based violence* is often used interchangeably with *violence against women*,[^cite_note-Krantz-15] and some articles on VAW reiterate these conceptions by stating that men are the main perpetrators of this violence.[^cite_note-16] Moreover, the definition stated by the 1993 Declaration on the Elimination of Violence Against Women also supported the notion that violence is rooted in the inequality between men and women when the term *violence* is used together with the term *gender-based*.[^cite_note-Krantz-15]

In *Recommendation Rec(2002)5 of the Committee of Ministers to member states on the protection of women against violence*, the Council of Europe stipulated that VAW "includes, but is not limited to, the following":[^cite_note-17]

a. violence occurring in the family or domestic unit, including, *inter alia*, physical and mental aggression, emotional and psychological abuse, rape and sexual abuse, incest, rape between spouses, regular or occasional partners and cohabitants, crimes committed in the name of honour, female genital and sexual mutilation and other traditional practices harmful to women such as forced marriages;

b. violence occurring within the general community, including, *inter alia*, rape, sexual abuse, sexual harassment and intimidation at work, in institutions or elsewhere; trafficking in women for the purposes of sexual exploitation, and economic exploitation and sex tourism;

c. violence perpetrated or condoned by the state or its officials;

d. violation of the human rights of women in situations of armed conflict, in particular the taking of hostages, forced displacement, systematic rape, sexual slavery, forced pregnancy, and trafficking for the purposes of sexual exploitation and economic exploitation.

These definitions of VAW as gender-based are seen by some to be unsatisfactory. These definitions understand society as patriarchal, signifying unequal relations between men and women.[^cite_note-18] Opponents of such definitions argue that the definitions disregard violence against men and that the term *[gender](38076.md)*, as used in *gender-based violence*, only refers to women. Other critics argue that employing the term *gender* in this particular way introduces notions of inferiority and subordination for femininity and superiority for masculinity.[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-Michau,_L._2007_pp.96-20] There is no widely accepted current definition that covers all the dimensions of gender-based violence.[^cite_note-Prügl,_E._2013-21]

| Document | Adopted by | Date | Definition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| General Recommendation 19 | [CEDAW Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CEDAW_Committee) | 1992 | 'The definition of discrimination includes gender-based violence, that is, violence that is directed against a woman because she is a woman or that affects women disproportionately.'[^cite_note-22] |
| [DEVAW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DEVAW) | [United Nations](31769.md) | 20 December 1993 | '...the term "violence against women" means any act of gender-based violence that results in, or is likely to result in, physical, sexual or psychological harm or suffering to women'.[^cite_note-DEVAW_text-23] |
| [Belém do Pará Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bel%C3%A9m_do_Par%C3%A1_Convention) | [Organization of American States](22788.md) | 9 June 1994 | '...violence against women shall be understood as any act or conduct, based on gender, which causes death or physical, sexual or psychological harm or suffering to women, whether in the public or the private sphere.'[^cite_note-24] |
| [Maputo Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maputo_Protocol) | [African Union](10634933.md) | 11 July 2003 | '"Violence against women" means all acts perpetrated against women which cause or could cause them physical, sexual, psychological, and economic harm, including the threat to take such acts; or to undertake the imposition of arbitrary restrictions on or deprivation of fundamental freedoms in private or public life in peace time and during situations of armed conflicts or of war...'[^cite_note-25] |
| [Istanbul Convention](31752276.md) | [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) | 11 May 2011 | '..."violence against women" is understood as a violation of human rights and a form of discrimination against women and shall mean all acts of gender-based violence that result in, or are likely to result in, physical, sexual, psychological or economic harm or suffering to women, including threats of such acts, coercion or arbitrary deprivation of liberty, whether occurring in public or in private life; ... "gender" shall mean the socially constructed roles, behaviours, activities and attributes that a given society considers appropriate for women and men; "gender-based violence against women" shall mean violence that is directed against a woman because she is a woman or that affects women disproportionately...'. The preamble notes: '...Recognising that women and girls are exposed to a higher risk of gender-based violence than men; Recognising that domestic violence affects women disproportionately, and that men may also be victims of domestic violence...'[^cite_note-26] |

### Forms of violence

Violence against women fits into several broad categories. These include violence carried out by individuals and states.

Some forms of violence by individual perpetrators are: [rape](7980471.md), [domestic violence](18978563.md), [sexual harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_harassment), [acid throwing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_throwing), [reproductive coercion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_coercion), [female infanticide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Female_infanticide), [prenatal sex selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex-selective_abortion), [obstetric violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obstetric_violence), [online gender-based violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_Gender-Based_Violence) and [mob violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mob_violence); as well as harmful customary or traditional practices such as [honor killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honor_killings), [dowry violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dowry_death), [female genital mutilation](11408.md), [marriage by abduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_by_abduction) and [forced marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_marriage).

Forms of violence may be perpetrated or condoned by governments, such as [war rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_rape); [sexual violence](731637.md) and [sexual slavery](173634.md) during conflict, [forced sterilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_sterilization); [forced abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_abortion); violence by police and authoritative personnel; [stoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoning) and [flogging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flogging).

Many forms of VAW, such as [trafficking in women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trafficking_in_women) and [forced prostitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_prostitution) are often perpetrated by organized criminal networks.[^cite_note-Prügl,_E._2013-21] Historically, there have been forms of organized WAV, such as [Witch trials in the early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witch_trials_in_the_early_modern_period) or the [sexual slavery](173634.md) of [comfort women](302767.md). The Gender Equality Commission of the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) identifies nine forms of violence against women based on subject and context rather than life cycle or time period:[^cite_note-Hagemann-White-27][^cite_note-28]

- 'Violence within the family or domestic violence'

- 'Rape and sexual violence'

- 'Sexual harassment'

- 'Violence in institutional environments'

- 'Female genital mutilation'

- 'Forced marriages'

- 'Violence in conflict and post-conflict situations'

- 'Killings in the name of honour'

- 'Failure to respect freedom of choice with regard to reproduction'

#### By age groups

The [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) has developed a typology of violence against women based on their cultural life cycles.

| **Phase** | **Type of violence** |
| --- | --- |
| Pre-birth | Sex-selective abortion; effects of battering during pregnancy on birth outcomes |
| Infancy | Female infanticide; physical, sexual and psychological abuse |
| Girlhood | Child marriage; female genital mutilation; physical, sexual and psychological abuse; incest; child prostitution and pornography |
| Adolescence and adulthood | Dating and courtship violence (e.g. acid throwing and date rape); economically coerced sex (e.g. school girls having sex with "sugar daddies" in return for school fees); incest; sexual abuse in the workplace; rape; sexual harassment; forced prostitution and pornography; trafficking in women; partner violence; marital rape; dowry abuse and murders; partner homicide; psychological abuse; abuse of women with disabilities; forced pregnancy |
| Elderly | Forced "suicide" or homicide of widows for economic reasons; sexual, physical and psychological abuse[^cite_note-WHO_VAW-29] |

## Sexual violence

Main articles: [Sexual violence](731637.md), [Sexual assault](218407.md), [Rape](7980471.md), and [Sexual harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_harassment)

[Sexual harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_harassment) involves actions with unwelcome sexual overtones, including verbal transgressions.[^cite_note-30] [Sexual violence](731637.md) is a broader term referring to violence to obtain a sexual act, for example, trafficking.[^cite_note-WHO_definition-31][^cite_note-icc-cpi.int-32] [Sexual assault](218407.md) is usually defined as unwanted sexual contact[^cite_note-Cameron-33] and when this involves [sexual penetration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_penetration) or [sexual intercourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_intercourse) it is referred to as [rape](7980471.md).

Women are most often the victims of rape, usually perpetrated by men known to them.[^cite_note-34] The rates of reporting, prosecution, and convictions for rape vary considerably in different jurisdictions and are influenced by the society's attitudes toward such crimes. It is considered the most underreported violent crime.[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-rds.homeoffice.gov.uk-36] Following a rape, a victim may face violence or threats from the rapist and, in many cultures, from the victim's own family and relatives. Violence or intimidation of the victim may be perpetrated by the rapist, or by friends and relatives of the rapist, as a way of preventing the victims from reporting the rape, punishing them for reporting it, or forcing them to withdraw the complaint; or it may be perpetrated by the relatives of the victim as a punishment for "bringing shame" to the family. Internationally, the incidence of rapes recorded by police during 2008 varied between 0.1 per 100,000 people in [Egypt](8087628.md) and 91.6 per 100,000 people in [Lesotho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesotho) with 4.9 per 100,000 people in [Lithuania](17675.md) as the [median](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median).[^cite_note-unodc1-37] Around the world, rape is often not reported or handled properly by law enforcement for a wide variety of reasons.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39]

In 2011, the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) found that "nearly 20% of all women" in the [United States](3434750.md) suffered attempted rape or rape sometime in their lives. More than a third of the victims were raped before the age of 18.[^cite_note-CDC2-40][^cite_note-NISVS20102-41]

### Survival sex

See also: [Survival sex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survival_sex) and [Violence against prostitutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence_against_prostitutes)

Some women engage in sex work out of necessity to meet basic needs, such as shelter and food. This is particularly prevalent among women experiencing homelessness.[^cite_note-Flowers_2010_110–112-42] Many women have said they were raped when engaging in sex work. They may be apprehensive about coming forward and reporting their attacks. When reported, many women have said that the stigma was too great, and that the police told them they deserved it and were reluctant to follow police policy. Decriminalizing sex work is argued to help sex workers in this aspect.[^cite_note-43]

In some countries, it is common for older men to engage in "compensated dating" with underage girls. Such relationships are called [enjo kōsai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enjo_k%C5%8Dsai) in Japan and are also common in Asian countries such as Taiwan, South Korea, and Hong Kong. The WHO condemned "economically coerced sex (e.g. school girls having sex with "[sugar daddies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_dating)" in return for school fees)" as a form of violence against women.[^cite_note-WHO_VAW-29]



[Kisaeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kisaeng) women from outcast or slave families.

Women from certain lower castes have been involved in prostitution as part of a tradition, called [Intergenerational prostitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_in_the_sex_industry). In pre-modern Korea, many women from the [lower caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_system_in_Korea) [Cheonmin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheonmin), known as [Kisaeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kisaeng), were trained to provide entertainment, conversation, and sexual services to upper-class men.[^cite_note-Cho103-44]

Women with illegal resident status are disproportionately involved with prostitution. For example, in 1997, *[Le Monde diplomatique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde_diplomatique)* stated that 80% of prostitutes in [Amsterdam](844.md) were foreigners and 70% had no immigration papers.[^cite_note-45]

### Forced sexual services

Main article: [Sexual slavery](173634.md)

#### By military forces

Main article: [Wartime sexual violence](17572536.md)



*[Brennus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brennus_\(leader_of_the_Senones\)) and His Share of the Spoils*, by [Paul Jamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Jamin), 1893



[Rangoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangoon), [Burma](19457.md). 8 August 1945. A young [ethnic Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_diaspora) woman from one of the [Imperial Japanese Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Japanese_Army)'s "comfort battalions" is interviewed by an [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) officer.

Militarism produces environments that allow for increased violence against women. [War rapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_rapes) have accompanied warfare in virtually every known historical era.[^cite_note-46] War rapes are rapes committed by soldiers, other combatants, or civilians during armed conflict, war, or military occupation, distinguished from sexual assaults and rapes committed amongst troops in military service. It also covers women forced into prostitution or [sexual slavery](173634.md) by an occupying power. During World War II, the Japanese military established brothels filled with "[comfort women](302767.md)", girls and women forced into sexual slavery for soldiers, exploiting women for the access and entitlement of men.[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48]

[Rape during the Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War) by members of the [Pakistani military](23369.md) and the [militias](20622.md) that supported them led to the rape of 200,000 women over a period of nine months. [Rape during the Bosnian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Bosnian_War) was used as a systematized instrument of war by Serb armed forces, predominantly targeting women and girls of the Bosniak ethnic group for physical and moral destruction. Estimates of the number of women raped during the war range from 50,000 to 60,000; as of 2010 only 12 cases have been prosecuted.[^cite_note-49]

The 1998 [International Criminal Tribunal for Rwanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Tribunal_for_Rwanda) recognized rape in the [Rwandan Genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rwandan_Genocide) as a war crime.[^cite_note-50]

According to one report, the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](9087364.md)'s capture of Iraqi cities in June 2014 was accompanied by an upsurge in crimes against women, including kidnapping and rape.[^cite_note-51] *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* reported that ISIL's extremist agenda extended to women's bodies and that women living under their control were being captured and raped.[^cite_note-52] Fighters are told that they are free to have sex and rape non-Muslim captive women.[^cite_note-53] Haleh Esfandiari from the [Woodrow Wilson International Center for Scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_International_Center_for_Scholars) highlighted abuse of local women by ISIL militants after they captured an area. "They usually take the older women to a makeshift slave market and try to sell them. The younger girls... are raped or married off to fighters," she said, adding, "It's based on temporary marriages, and once these fighters have had sex with these young girls, they just pass them on to other fighters."[^cite_note-54] In December 2014 the [Iraqi Ministry of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Human_Rights_\(Iraq\)) announced that the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](9087364.md) had killed over 150 women and girls in [Fallujah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallujah) who refused to participate in [sexual jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_jihad).[^cite_note-55]

During the [Rohingya genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_genocide) (2016–present), the [Armed Forces of Myanmar](20394.md), along with the Myanmar [Border Guard Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Police) and Buddhist militias of [Rakhine](493373.md), committed widespread gang rapes and other forms of sexual violence against the [Rohingya](1918632.md) Muslim women and girls. A January 2018 study estimated that the military and local Rakhine Buddhists perpetrated gang rapes and other sexual violence against 18,000 Rohingya Muslim women and girls.[^cite_note-56] The [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) stated that gang rapes and sexual violence were committed as part of the military's ethnic cleansing campaign, while the United Nations Special Representative of the Secretary General on Sexual Violence in Conflict, Pramila Patten, said that Rohingya women and girls were made the ["systematic" target of rapes and sexual violence because of their ethnic identity and religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocidal_rape). Other forms of sexual violence included [sexual slavery](173634.md) in military captivity, forced public nudity, and humiliation.[^cite_note-57] Some women and girls were raped to death, while others were found traumatised with raw wounds upon arrival in refugee camps in Bangladesh. Human Rights Watch reported a 15-year-old girl was ruthlessly dragged on the ground for over 50 feet and then was raped by 10 Burmese soldiers.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59]

#### By criminal groups



Countries by prevalence of female trafficking

Main articles: [Human trafficking](19333662.md) and [Forced prostitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_prostitution)

Human trafficking refers to the acquisition of persons by improper means such as force, fraud or deception, with the aim of [exploiting](40067623.md) them.[^cite_note-60] The [Protocol to Prevent, Suppress and Punish Trafficking in Persons, especially Women and Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protocol_to_Prevent,_Suppress_and_Punish_Trafficking_in_Persons,_especially_Women_and_Children) states,[^cite_note-61]

> "Trafficking in persons" shall mean the recruitment, transportation, transfer, harbouring or receipt of persons, by means of the threat or use of force or other forms of coercion, of abduction, of fraud, of deception, of the abuse of power or of a position of vulnerability or of the giving or receiving of payments or benefits to achieve the consent of a person having control over another person, for the purpose of exploitation. Exploitation shall include, at a minimum, the exploitation of the prostitution of others or other forms of sexual exploitation, forced labour or services, slavery or practices similar to slavery, servitude or the removal of organs.

Because of the illegal nature of trafficking, reliable data on its extent is limited.[^cite_note-apps.who.int-62] The [WHO](33583.md) states "Current evidence strongly suggests that those who are trafficked into the [sex industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_industry) and as domestic servants are more likely to be women and children."[^cite_note-apps.who.int-62] A 2006 study in Europe on trafficked women found that the women were subjected to serious forms of abuse, such as physical or sexual violence, affecting their physical and mental health.[^cite_note-apps.who.int-62]

[Forced prostitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_prostitution) is [prostitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostitution) resulting from [coercion](6512.md) by a third party. In forced prostitution, the party/parties who force the victim to be subjected to unwanted sexual acts exercise coercive control over the victim.[^cite_note-63]

## Domestic violence

### Violence related to acquiring a partner

See also: [Stalking](25272420.md) and [Acid throwing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_throwing)

Single and economically independent women have been vilified by certain groups of men. In [Hassi Messaoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassi_Messaoud) in [Algeria](358.md) in 2001, [mobs targeted single women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassi_Messaoud_mob_attacks_against_women), attacking 95 and killing at least six[^cite_note-Amnesty-64][^cite_note-65] and, in 2011, similar attacks happened again throughout Algeria.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67]

Stalking is unwanted or obsessive attention by an individual or group toward another person, often manifested through persistent [harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harassment), [intimidation](494675.md), or following/monitoring of the victim. Stalking is often understood as "a course of conduct directed at a specific person that would cause a reasonable person to feel fear.".[^cite_note-68] Although stalkers are frequently portrayed as strangers, they are most often known people, such as former or current partners, friends, colleagues or acquaintances. In the U.S., a survey by NVAW found that only 23% of female victims were stalked by strangers.[^cite_note-victimsofcrime.org-69] Stalking by partners can be very dangerous, as it sometimes escalates into severe violence, including murder.[^cite_note-victimsofcrime.org-69] Police statistics from the 1990s in Australia indicated that 87.7% of stalking offenders were male and 82.4% of stalking victims were female.[^cite_note-70]



Women are the overwhelming majority of victims of [acid attacks](30862640.md) and it is often motivated by rejection and jealousy.

An [acid attack](30862640.md) is the act of throwing acid at someone with the intention of injuring or disfiguring them. Women and girls are the victims in 75–80% of cases[^cite_note-71] and are often connected to domestic disputes, including dowry disputes, refusal of a marriage proposal, or sexual advances.[^cite_note-72] The acid is usually thrown at the face, burning tissue, often exposing and sometimes dissolving the bones.[^cite_note-73] The long-term consequences of these attacks include [blindness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blindness) and permanent [scarring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scarring) of the face and body.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75] Such attacks are most common in South Asia, in countries such as Bangladesh, Pakistan, and India; and in Southeast Asia, especially in Cambodia.[^cite_note-76]

#### Forced marriage

Main article: [Forced marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_marriage)

See also: [Bride kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_kidnapping)



The painting depicts a Chilean woman being kidnapped during a [malón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mal%C3%B3n). [Bride kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_kidnapping) for the purpose of [forced marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_marriage) and [forced pregnancy](37569084.md) was common through the history in many countries.



Criticism of the [Azeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijani_people) custom of [forced marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_marriage), as depicted in the early 20th-century satirical periodical [Molla Nasraddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molla_Nasraddin_\(magazine\))

A [forced marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_marriage) is a marriage against the will of one or both parties. Forced marriages are most common in South Asia, the Middle East, and Africa. The customs of [bride price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_price) and [dowry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dowry), which exist in many parts of the world, contribute to this practice. A forced marriage is often the result of a dispute between families, where the dispute is 'resolved' by giving a female from one family to the other.[^cite_note-77]

The custom of [bride kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_kidnapping) continues in some Central Asian countries, such as Kyrgyzstan, Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan, and the Caucasus, or in parts of Africa, especially Ethiopia. A girl or woman is abducted by the would-be groom, often helped by his friends. The victim is often raped by the would-be groom, after which he may try to negotiate a bride price with village elders to legitimize the marriage.[^cite_note-78]

Forced and child marriages are practiced by some inhabitants of Tanzania. Girls are sold by their families to older men for financial benefits, and often girls are married off as soon as they hit puberty, which can be as young as seven years old.[^cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-2014-79] To the older men, these young brides act as symbols of masculinity and accomplishment. Child brides endure forced sex, causing health risks and growth impediments.[^cite_note-80] Primary education is usually not completed for young girls in forced marriages. Married and pregnant students are often discriminated against, expelled, and excluded from school.[^cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-2014-79] The Law of Marriage Act currently does not address issues with guardianship and child marriage. The issue of child marriage is not addressed enough in this law, and only establishes a minimum age of 18 for the men of Tanzania.[^cite_note-81]

#### Dowry violence

Main articles: [Dowry death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dowry_death) and [Bride burning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_burning)



Anti-dowry poster in [Bangalore, India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangalore,_India)

The custom of [dowry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dowry), which is common in South Asia, especially in India, is the trigger of many forms of violence against women. [Bride burning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_burning) is the killing of a bride at home by her husband or husband's family, due to his dissatisfaction over the dowry provided by her family. [Dowry death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dowry_death) refers to women and girls being killed or committing [suicide](16831059.md) due to disputes regarding dowry. Dowry violence is common in India, Pakistan, Bangladesh and Nepal. In India, in 2011 alone, the National Crime Records Bureau reported 8,618 dowry deaths, while unofficial figures suggest the numbers to be at least three times higher.[^cite_note-82]

### Violence within a relationship

Main article: [Intimate partner violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intimate_partner_violence)



Anti-domestic violence sign in [Liberia](17791.md)

See also: [Coverture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coverture) and [Criticism of marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_marriage)

The relation between violence against women and marriage laws, regulations and traditions has also been discussed.[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] Roman law gave men the right to punish their wives, even to the point of death.[^cite_note-Roman_Law-85] The US and English law subscribed until the 20th century to the system of [coverture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coverture), a legal doctrine where upon marriage, a woman's legal rights were subsumed by her husband's.[^cite_note-86] Common-law in the United States and in the UK allowed for domestic violence[^cite_note-87] and in the UK, before 1891, the husband had the right to inflict moderate [corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_punishment) on his wife to keep her "within the bounds of duty".[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] Today many countries severely restrict the rights of married women: for example, in [Yemen](350939.md), marriage regulations state that a wife must obey her husband and must not leave home without his permission.[^cite_note-90] In [Iraq](7515928.md) husbands have a legal right to "punish" their wives. The criminal code states in Paragraph 41 that there is no crime, if an act is committed while exercising a legal right; examples of such legal rights include: "The punishment of a wife by her husband, the disciplining by parents and teachers of children under their authority within certain limits prescribed by law or by custom".[^cite_note-91] In the West, married women faced discrimination until some decades ago: for instance, in France, married women received the right to work without their husband's permission in 1965.[^cite_note-92] In Spain, during the Franco era, a married woman required her husband's consent (*permiso marital*) for nearly all economic activities, including employment, ownership of property and traveling away from home; the *permiso marital* was abolished in 1975.[^cite_note-93] Concerns exist about violence related to marriage – both inside marriage (physical abuse, sexual violence, restriction of liberty) and in relation to marriage customs ([dowry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dowry), [bride price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_price), [forced marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_marriage), [child marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_marriage), [marriage by abduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_by_abduction), violence related to female premarital [virginity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginity)). Claudia Card, professor of philosophy at the University of Wisconsin-Madison, writes:[^cite_note-94]

The legal rights of access that married partners have to each other's persons, property, and lives makes it all but impossible for a spouse to defend herself (or himself), or to be protected against torture, rape, battery, stalking, mayhem, or murder by the other spouse... Legal marriage thus enlists state support for conditions conducive to murder and mayhem.

#### Physical



An illustration from [JJ Grandville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Ignace_Isidore_G%C3%A9rard)'s *Cent Proverbes* (1845) captioned "Qui aime bien châtie bien" (*Who loves well, punishes well*). A man beating a woman is shown in the back.

Women are more likely to be [victimized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victimized) by someone that they are intimate with, commonly called "[intimate partner violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intimate_partner_violence)" (IPV). IPV is often unreported to police and making it difficult to estimate the true magnitude of the problem.[^cite_note-95] Though this violence is often considered only an issue within heterosexual relationships, it also occurs in lesbian relationships,[^cite_note-96] daughter-mother relationships, [roommate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roommate) relationships and other domestic relationships involving women. Violence against women in lesbian relationships is about as common as violence against women in heterosexual relationships.[^cite_note-97]

Women are much more likely than men to be [murdered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murdered) by an intimate partner. In the United States, in 2005, 1181 women were killed by intimate partners, compared to 329 men.[^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99] An estimated 30% or more of women who are admitted to the ER could be victims of domestic violence.[^cite_note-100]

In [England and Wales](275187.md) about 100 women are killed by partners or former partners each year, while 21 men were killed in 2010.[^cite_note-101] In 2008, in France, 156 women were killed by their intimate partner, compared to 27 men.[^cite_note-102] According to the WHO, globally, as many as 38% of murders of women are committed by an intimate partner.[^cite_note-WHO_VAM_Factsheet-103] A UN report compiled from a number of different studies conducted in at least 71 countries found domestic violence against women to be most prevalent in [Ethiopia](187749.md).[^cite_note-104] A study by [Pan American Health Organization](1246706.md) conducted in 12 [Latin American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American) countries found the highest prevalence of domestic violence against women to be in [Bolivia](3462.md).[^cite_note-105] Finland has received major international criticism for the legal process for violence against women; with authors noting that a high level of equality for women in the public sphere (as in Finland) should never be equated with equality in all other aspects of women's lives.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108]

The [American Psychiatric Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Psychiatric_Association) planning and research committees for the forthcoming [DSM-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DSM-5) (2013) have canvassed a series of new [Relational disorders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relational_disorder), which include *Marital Conflict Disorder Without Violence* or *Marital Abuse Disorder (Marital Conflict Disorder With Violence)*.[^cite_note-Personality-109]<sup>: 164, 166 </sup> Couples with marital disorders sometimes come to clinical attention because the couple recognize long-standing dissatisfaction with their marriage and come to the [clinician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinician) on their own initiative, or are referred by a health care professional. Alternatively, there is serious violence in the marriage that is "usually the husband battering the wife".[^cite_note-Personality-109]<sup>: 163 </sup> In these cases the emergency room or a legal authority often is the first to notify the [clinician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinician). Most importantly, marital violence "is a major risk factor for serious injury and even death and women in violent marriages are at much greater risk of being seriously injured or killed ([National Advisory Council on Violence Against Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Advisory_Council_on_Violence_Against_Women) 2000)".[^cite_note-Personality-109]<sup>: 166 </sup>

The authors conclude with "very recent information"[^cite_note-Personality-109]<sup>: 167, 168 </sup> on the course of violent marriages, suggesting that "over time a husband's battering may abate somewhat, but perhaps because he has successfully [intimidated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intimidate) his wife. The risk of violence remains strong in a marriage in which it has been a feature in the past. Thus, treatment is essential here; the clinician cannot just wait and watch."[^cite_note-Personality-109]<sup>: 167, 168 </sup> The most urgent clinical priority is the protection of the wife because she is most frequently at risk, and clinicians must be aware that supporting assertiveness by a battered wife may lead to more beatings or even death.[^cite_note-Personality-109]<sup>: 167, 168 </sup>

#### Sexual

Main articles: [Intimate partner sexual violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intimate_partner_sexual_violence), [Date rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_rape), and [Marital rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marital_rape)

Further information: [Dating abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dating_abuse) and [Teen dating violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teen_dating_violence)

Marital or spousal rape was once widely condoned or ignored by law and is now widely considered unacceptable violence against women, repudiated by international conventions, and increasingly criminalized. Still, in many countries, spousal rape remains legal or is illegal but widely tolerated and accepted as a husband's prerogative. The criminalization of spousal rape is recent, having occurred during the past few decades. Traditional understanding and views of [marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage), [rape](7980471.md), [sexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexuality), [gender roles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_roles) and [self determination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self_determination) have started to be challenged in most Western countries during the 1960s and 1970s, leading to the criminalization of marital rape in the following decades. With a few notable exceptions, it was during the past 30 years that most laws against marital rape have been enacted. Some countries in Scandinavia and in the former Communist Bloc of Europe made spousal rape illegal before 1970, but most Western countries criminalized it only in the 1980s and 1990s. In many parts of the world, laws against marital rape are very new, having been enacted in the 2000s.[^cite_note-110]



&#x20; [Marital rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marital_rape) illegal

&#x20; Marital rape legal

&#x20; 'Marital rape' legal, but forced marital sex still punishable.

&#x20; Legal status unclear

In Canada, marital rape was made illegal in 1983, when several legal changes were made, including changing the rape statute to *sexual assault* and making the laws gender neutral.[^cite_note-grady-111][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] In Ireland, spousal rape was outlawed in 1990.[^cite_note-Temkin86-114] In the US, the criminalization of marital rape started in the mid-1970s, and in 1993, North Carolina became the last state to make marital rape illegal.[^cite_note-115] In [England and Wales](275187.md), marital rape was made illegal in 1991. The views of Sir Matthew Hale, a 17th-century jurist, published in *The History of the Pleas of the Crown* (1736), stated that a husband cannot be guilty of the rape of his wife because the wife "hath given up herself in this kind to her husband, which she cannot retract"; in England and Wales, this would remain law for more than 250 years, until it was abolished by the [Appellate Committee of the House of Lords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appellate_Committee_of_the_House_of_Lords), in the case of *R v. R* in 1991.[^cite_note-116] In the Netherlands, marital rape was also made illegal in 1991.[^cite_note-117] One of the last Western countries to criminalize marital rape was Germany, in 1997.[^cite_note-118]

Dating abuse, or dating violence, is the perpetration of coercion, intimidation, or assault in the context of [dating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dating) or [courtship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courtship). It is also when one partner tries to maintain [abusive power and control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abusive_power_and_control). Dating violence is defined by the CDC as "the physical, sexual, psychological, or emotional violence within a dating relationship, including stalking".[^cite_note-119]

### Religious



A member of the [Taliban](30635.md)'s [religious police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutaween#Other_groups) beating a woman in [Kabul](16826.md), 2001 showing the [treatment of women by the Taliban](31285.md)

See also: [Christianity and domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_and_domestic_violence) and [Islam and domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_domestic_violence)

The relationship between [religious violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_violence) and gender is controversial. The [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) at 1 Corinthians 7\:3-5 explains that one has a "conjugal duty" to have sexual relations with one's spouse (in sharp opposition to [sex outside marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_outside_marriage), which is considered a [sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin)) and states, "The wife does not have authority over her own body, but the husband does. And likewise, the husband does not have authority over his own body, but the wife does. Do not deprive one another."[^cite_note-120] Some conservative religious figures interpret this as rejecting to possibility of marital rape.[^cite_note-121] Islam makes reference to sexual relations in marriage too, notably: "Allah's Apostle said, 'If a husband calls his wife to his bed (i.e. to have sexual relation) and she refuses and causes him to sleep in anger, the angels will curse her till morning';"[^cite_note-122] and several comments on the issue of marital rape made by Muslim religious leaders have been criticized.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124]

### Widowhood related violence



[Sati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sati_\(practice\)) (a Hindu practice whereby a [widow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widow) immolates herself on the funeral pyre of her husband) ceremony



A 1533 account of the execution of a witch charged with burning the [German](11867.md) town of [Schiltach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schiltach) in 1531

See also: [Widow § Abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widow#Abuse), [Widow inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widow_inheritance), and [Sexual rites of passage § Sexual cleansing of widows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_rites_of_passage#Sexual_cleansing_of_widows)

Widows have been subjected to forced remarriage called [widow inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widow_inheritance), where they are forced to marry a male relative of their late husband.[^cite_note-125] Another practice is the banned remarriage of widows, which is legal in India[^cite_note-peers-2006-pp52-53-126] and Korea.[^cite_note-uhn-127] A more extreme version is the ritual killing of widows, as seen in India and Fiji. [Sati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sati_\(practice\)) is the burning of widows, and although sati in India is today an almost defunct practice, isolated incidents have occurred in recent years, such as the 1987 sati of [Roop Kanwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roop_Kanwar), as well as several incidents in rural areas in 2002[^cite_note-128] and 2006.[^cite_note-129] A traditional idea, upheld in some places in Africa, is that an unmarried widow is unholy and "disturbed", if she is unmarried and abstains from sex for some period of time. This fuels the practice of [widow cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_rites_of_passage#Sexual_cleansing_of_widows) where the unmarried widow is required to have sexual intercourse as a form of ritual purification, which is commenced with a ceremony for the neighborhood to witness that she is now purified.[^cite_note-farmradio-130]

Unmarried widows are most likely to be accused and killed as [witches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_witch-hunts).[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-Film_script-132] [Witch trials in the early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witch_trials_in_the_early_modern_period) (between the 15th and 18th centuries) were common in Europe and in the European colonies in North America. Today, there remain regions of the world (such as parts of [Sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md), rural [North India](1552939.md), and [Papua New Guinea](22943.md)) where belief in [witchcraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witchcraft) is held by many people, and women accused of being witches are subjected to serious violence.[^cite_note-133]

## Non-intimate partner family violence

### Infanticide and abandonment



India's child sex ratio, 2011



Birth sex ratios, 2012



Chinese anti-infanticide tract circa 1800. China has a long history of son preference, which was aggravated after the enforcement of the [one child policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_child_policy).

*Son preference* is a custom prevalent in many societies,[^cite_note-StopVAW_son-134] that in its extreme can lead to the rejection of daughters. [Sex-selective abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex-selective_abortion) of females is more common among the higher income population, who can access [medical technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assisted_reproductive_technology). After birth, neglect and diverting resources to male children can lead to some countries having a skewed ratio with more boys than girls,[^cite_note-StopVAW_son-134] with such practices killing an approximate 230,000 girls under five in India each year.[^cite_note-135] In China, the [one child policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_child_policy) increased sex-selective abortions and was largely responsible for an unbalanced sex ratio.*[The Dying Rooms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dying_Rooms)* is a 1995 [television](29831.md) [documentary film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Documentary_film) about Chinese state orphanages, which documented how parents abandoned their newborn girls into [orphanages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orphanages), where the staff would leave the children in rooms to die of thirst, or starvation.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137]

Another manifestation of son preference is the violence inflicted against mothers who give birth to girls.[^cite_note-138]

### Body modification

#### Genitalia

Main articles: [Female genital mutilation](11408.md) and [labia stretching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labia_stretching)



Data map showing the percentage of women aged 15 to 49 who have undergone FGM (2020). There is no data for countries in light grey.

[^cite_note-139]



Anti-[FGM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FGM) road sign, Bakau, Gambia, 2005

Female genital mutilation (FGM) is defined by the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) as "all procedures that involve partial or total removal of the external female genitalia or other injury to the female genital organs for non-medical reasons".[^cite_note-WHO_FGM-140]

The WHO states: "The procedure has no health benefits for girls and women" and "Procedures can cause severe bleeding and problems urinating, and later cysts, infections, infertility, and complications in childbirth increase the risk of newborn deaths".[^cite_note-WHO_FGM-140]

According to a 2016 UNICEF report, the top rates for FGM are in Somalia (with 98 percent of women affected), Guinea (97 percent), Djibouti (93 percent), Egypt (87 percent), Eritrea (83 percent), Mali (89 percent), Sierra Leone (90 percent), Sudan (87 percent), Gambia (75 percent), Burkina Faso (76 percent), Ethiopia (74 percent), Mauritania (69 percent), Liberia (50 percent), and Guinea-Bissau (45 percent).[^cite_note-\:1-141] More than half of the cases documented by UNICEF are concentrated in just three countries (Indonesia, Egypt and Ethiopia).[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-\:1-141]

FGM is linked to cultural rites and customs, including traditional practices, and religion. It continues to take place in different communities in Africa and the Middle East, including in places where it is banned by national legislation. According to a 2016 UNICEF report, at least 200 million of women and girls in Africa and the Middle East have experienced FGM.[^cite_note-\:1-141] Due to globalization and immigration, FGM is spreading beyond the borders of Africa and the Middle East to countries such as Australia, Belgium, Canada, France, New Zealand, the U.S., and UK.[^cite_note-143]

Although FGM is today associated with developing countries, this practice was common until the 1970s in parts of the Western world, too. FGM was considered a standard medical procedure in the United States for most of the 19th and 20th centuries.[^cite_note-Webber-2003-144] Physicians performed surgeries of varying invasiveness to treat a number of diagnoses, including [hysteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hysteria), [depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depression_\(mood\)), [nymphomania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nymphomania), and [frigidity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frigidity).[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-Webber-2003-144]

As of 2016, in Africa, FGM has been legally banned in Benin, Burkina Faso, the Central African Republic, Chad, Côte d'Ivoire, Djibouti, Egypt, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Gambia, Ghana, Guinea, Guinea Bissau, Kenya, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal, South Africa, Tanzania, Togo, Uganda, and Zambia.[^cite_note-146] The [Istanbul Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_preventing_and_combating_violence_against_women_and_domestic_violence) prohibits female genital mutilation (Article 38).[^cite_note-147]

[Labia stretching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labia_stretching) is the act of lengthening the *[labia minora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labia_minora)* (the inner lips of the female genitals) through manual manipulation (pulling) or equipment (such as weights).[^cite_note-Rwanda-148] It is often done by older women to girls.[^cite_note-Beeb-149]

#### Feet



X-ray of [bound feet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bound_feet), China

[Foot-binding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foot-binding) was a practice in China done to reduce the size of feet in girls. It was seen as more desirable and was likely to make a more prestigious marriage.[^cite_note-150]

#### Force-feeding

Main article: [leblouh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leblouh)

In some countries, notably Mauritania, young girls are forcibly fattened to prepare them for marriage because obesity is seen as desirable. This practice of [force-feeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Force-feeding) is known as *[leblouh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leblouh)* or *gavage*.[^cite_note-151] The practice dates back to the 11th century, and has been reported to have made a significant comeback after a [military junta took over the country in 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Mauritanian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat).[^cite_note-152]

### Sexual initiation rites

Main article: [Sexual rites of passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_rites_of_passage)

Sexual "cleansing" is a ceremony where girls have sexual intercourse as a cleansing ritual following their first menstruation[^cite_note-bbc-153] and is referred to as *kusasa fumbi* in some regions of Malawi.[^cite_note-154] Prepubescent girls are often sent to a training camp where women known as *anamkungwi*, or "key leaders," teach the girls how to cook, clean, and have sexual intercourse in order to be wives.[^cite_note-theatlantic-155] After the training, a man known as a hyena performs the cleansing for 12- to 17-year-old females for three days and the girl is sometimes required to perform a bare-breasted dance, known as *chisamba*, to signal the end of her initiation in front of the community.[^cite_note-theguardian-156]

### Honor killings

[Honor killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honor_killings) are a common form of violence against women in certain parts of the world. Honor killings are perpetrated by family members (usually husbands, fathers, uncles, or brothers) against women in the family who are believed to have caused dishonor to the family. The death of the dishonorable woman is believed to restore honor.[^cite_note-157] These killings are a traditional practice believed to have originated from tribal customs, where an allegation against a woman can be enough to defile a family's reputation.[^cite_note-BBC_ethics-158][^cite_note-159] Women are killed for reasons such as refusing to enter an [arranged marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arranged_marriage), being in a relationship that is disapproved by their relatives, attempting to leave a marriage, having sex outside marriage, becoming the victim of rape, and dressing in ways that are deemed inappropriate, among others.[^cite_note-BBC_ethics-158][^cite_note-bbc.co.uk-160] In cultures where female [virginity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginity) is highly valued and considered mandatory before marriage, in extreme cases, rape victims are killed in [honor killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honor_killings). Victims may also be forced by their families to marry the rapist in order to restore the family's "honor.".[^cite_note-161] In Lebanon, the [Campaign Against Lebanese Rape Law - Article 522](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_Against_Lebanese_Rape_Law_-_Article_522) was launched in December 2016 to abolish the article that permitted a rapist to escape prison by marrying his victim. In Italy, before 1981, the Criminal Code provided for mitigating circumstances in case of a killing of a woman or her sexual partner for reasons related to honor, providing for a reduced sentence.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163]

Honor killings are common in countries such as Afghanistan, Egypt, Iraq, Jordan, Lebanon, Libya, Morocco, Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, Syria, Turkey, and Yemen.[^cite_note-bbc.co.uk-160][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167] Honor killings also occur in immigrant communities in Europe, the United States, and Canada. Although honor killings are most often associated with the Middle East and South Asia, they occur in other parts of the world too.[^cite_note-BBC_ethics-158][^cite_note-168] In India, honor killings occur in the northern regions of the country, especially in the states of Punjab, Haryana, Bihar, Uttar Pradesh, Rajasthan, Jharkhand, Himachal Pradesh and Madhya Pradesh.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170] In Turkey, honor killings are a serious problem in Southeastern Anatolia.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172]

## Pregnancy-related violence



[Maternal Mortality Rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_Mortality_Rate) worldwide, as defined by the number of maternal deaths per 100,000 live births from any cause related to or aggravated by pregnancy or its management, excluding accidental or incidental causes.

[^cite_note-CIA2010-173]

See also: [Domestic violence and pregnancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence_and_pregnancy) and [Reproductive coercion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_coercion)

[Obstetric violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obstetric_violence) refers to acts categorized as physically or psychologically violent in the context of labor and birth. A pregnant woman can sometimes be coerced into accepting surgical interventions that are done without her consent.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-WHO_Normal-175] This could include the "[husband's stitch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husband's_stitch)" in which one or more additional sutures than necessary are used to repair a woman's perineum after it has been torn or cut during childbirth with the intent of tightening the opening of the vagina and thereby enhancing the pleasure of her male sex partner during penetrative intercourse. Several Latin American countries have laws to protect against obstetric violence.[^cite_note-176] [Reproductive coercion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_coercion) is a collection of behaviors that interfere with decision-making related to [reproductive health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_health).[^cite_note-177] According to the WHO, "discrimination in health care settings takes many forms and is often manifested when an individual or group is denied access to health care services that are otherwise available to others. It can also occur through denial of services that are only needed by certain groups, such as women."[^cite_note-178]

### Restrictions around menstruation



Awareness raising through education is taking place among young girls to modify or eliminate the practice of chhaupadi in Nepal.

See also: [Chhaupadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chhaupadi)

Women in some cultures are forced into [social isolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_isolation) during their [menstrual periods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menstruation). In parts of Nepal, for instance, they are forced to live in sheds, are forbidden to touch men or even to enter the courtyard of their own homes, and are barred from consuming milk, yogurt, butter, meat, and various other foods, for fear they will contaminate those goods. Women have died during this period because of starvation, bad weather, or bites by snakes.[^cite_note-179] In cultures where women are restricted from being in public places, by law or custom, women who break such restrictions often face violence.[^cite_note-180]

### Forced pregnancy

Main article: [Forced pregnancy](37569084.md)

[Forced pregnancy](37569084.md) is the practice of forcing a woman or girl to become [pregnant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pregnant). A common motivation is to help establish a [forced marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_marriage), including by means of [bride kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_kidnapping). This was also used as part of a program of breeding [slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave) (see [Slave breeding in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_breeding_in_the_United_States)). In the 20th century, state mandated forced marriage with the aim of increasing the population was practiced by some authoritarian governments, notably during the [Khmer Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Rouge) regime in [Cambodia](334751.md), which systematically forced people into marriages, ordering them to have children, in order to increase the population and continue the revolution.[^cite_note-181]

The issue of *forced continuation of pregnancy* (i.e. denying a woman safe and legal abortion) is also seen by some organizations as a violation of women's rights. For example, the [Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_the_Elimination_of_Discrimination_against_Women) considers the criminalization of abortion a "violations of women's sexual and reproductive health and rights" and a form of "gender based violence".[^cite_note-182]

In addition, in some parts of Latin America, with very strict anti-abortion laws, pregnant women avoid the medical system due to fear of being investigated by the authorities if they have a miscarriage, or a stillbirth, or other problems with the pregnancy. Prosecuting such women is quite common in places such as [El Salvador](9356.md).[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184][^cite_note-ReferenceB-185][^cite_note-reuters.com-186]

### Forced sterilization and forced abortion

Main articles: [Forced sterilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_sterilization) and [Forced abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_abortion)



A map from a 1929 Swedish royal commission report displays the U.S. states that had implemented sterilization legislation by then

[Forced sterilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_sterilization) and [forced abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_abortion) are considered forms of gender-based violence.[^cite_note-187] The [Istanbul Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_preventing_and_combating_violence_against_women_and_domestic_violence) prohibits forced abortion and [forced sterilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_sterilization).[^cite_note-188] According to the Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination Against Women, all "women are guaranteed the right to decide freely and responsibly on the number of and spacing of their children, and to have access to information, education, and means to enable them to exercise these rights."[^cite_note-189]

Studies show forced sterilizations often target socially and politically disadvantaged groups such as racial and ethnic minorities, the poor, and indigenous populations.[^cite_note-Volscho,_Thomas_W_2010-190] In the United States, much of the history of forced sterilization is connected to the legacy of eugenics and [racism in the United States](2162835.md).[^cite_note-Volscho,_Thomas_W_2010-190] Many doctors thought that they were doing the country a service by sterilizing women who were poor, disabled, or a minority; the doctors considered those women to be a drain on the system.[^cite_note-Volscho,_Thomas_W_2010-190][^cite_note-North_Carolina_Sterilization_Program_2011-191] [Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States), [Mexican American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans), [African American](2154.md) and [Puerto Rican-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rican-American) women were coerced into sterilization programs, with Native Americans and African Americans especially being targeted.[^cite_note-Volscho,_Thomas_W_2010-190] Records have shown that Native American girls as young as eleven years-old had [hysterectomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hysterectomy) operations performed.[^cite_note-192]

In Europe, there have been a number of lawsuits and accusations towards the [Czech Republic](5321.md) and [Slovakia](26830.md) of [sterilizing Roma women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterilization_of_Romani_women) without adequate information and waiting period.[^cite_note-193] In response, both nations have instituted a mandatory seven-day waiting period and written consent. Slovakia has been condemned on the issue of forced sterilization of Roma women several times by the [European Court for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_for_Human_Rights) (see *[V. C. vs. Slovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V._C._vs._Slovakia)*, *[N. B. vs. Slovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N._B._vs._Slovakia)* and *[I.G. and Others vs. Slovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I.G._and_Others_vs._Slovakia)*).[^cite_note-194]

In [Peru](170691.md), in 1995, [Alberto Fujimori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Fujimori) launched a family planning initiative that especially targeted poor and indigenous women. In total, over 215,000 women were sterilized, with over 200,000 believed to have been coerced.[^cite_note-195] In 2002, Health Minister [Fernando Carbone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_carbone?action=edit\&redlink=1) admitted that the government gave misleading information, offered food incentives, and threatened to fine parents if they had additional children. The procedures have also been found to have been negligent, with less than half using proper anesthetic.[^cite_note-196]

In China, the [one child policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_child_policy) included forced abortions and forced sterilization.[^cite_note-197] Forced sterilization is also practiced in Uzbekistan.[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199]

## Women-specific state restrictions

### Dress

In [Iran](75225374.md), since 1981, after the [Islamic Revolution](347268.md), all women are required to wear loose-fitting clothing and a headscarf in public.[^cite_note-Ramezani10-200][^cite_note-Milani-201] In 1983, the Islamic Consultative Assembly decided that women who do not cover their hair in public will be punished with 74 lashes. Since 1995, unveiled women can also be imprisoned for up to 60 days.[^cite_note-202] The [Iranian protests against compulsory hijab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_protests_against_compulsory_hijab) continued into the [September 2022 Iranian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_2022_Iranian_protests), triggered in response to the killing of [Mahsa Amini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahsa_Amini), who was allegedly beaten to death by police due to wearing an "improper hijab". In [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), after the Grand Mosque seizure of 1979, it became mandatory for women to veil in public[^cite_note-203] but this is no longer required since 2018.[^cite_note-204] In Afghanistan, since May 2022, women are required to wear a hijab and face covering in public.[^cite_note-205] In countries without mandatory hijab women can face harassment and victim blaming due to not wearing a [hijab](68301.md).[^cite_note-a604-206]

The hijab has seen bans in places such as Austria,[^cite_note-207] Yugoslavia,[^cite_note-208] Kosovo,[^cite_note-209] Kazakhstan,[^cite_note-210] the Soviet Union,[^cite_note-Subaltern-211] and Tunisia.[^cite_note-tunisiahijab-212] On 8 January 1936,[^cite_note-213] [Reza Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Shah) issued a decree, [Kashf-e hijab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashf-e_hijab), banning all veils.[^cite_note-Milani-201] To enforce this decree, the police were ordered to physically remove the veil from any woman who wore it in public. Women who refused were beaten, their headscarves and [chadors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chador) torn off, and their homes forcibly searched.[^cite_note-Hoodfar-214]

### Freedom of movement

Women are, in many parts of the world, severely restricted in their [freedom of movement](1270497.md). Freedom of movement is an essential right, recognized by international instruments, including Article 15 (4) of [CEDAW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CEDAW).[^cite_note-215] Nevertheless, in some countries, women are not legally allowed to leave home without a [male guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali_\(Islamic_legal_guardian\)) (male relative or husband).[^cite_note-216] Saudi Arabia was the only country in the world where women were [forbidden to drive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_to_drive_movement) motor vehicles until June 2018.[^cite_note-bashraheel-217]

### Sexuality

Sex crimes such as adultery and [sex outside marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_outside_marriage) are disproportionately levelled against women and the punishment is often [stoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoning) and [flogging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flogging). This has been seen in Iran, Saudi Arabia, Sudan, Pakistan, Yemen, the United Arab Emirates, and some states in Nigeria.[^cite_note-218] Additionally this can deter victims of [sexual violence](731637.md) from reporting the crime, because the victims may themselves be punished (if they cannot prove their case, if they are deemed to have been in the company of an unrelated male, or if they were unmarried and not virgins at the time of the rape).[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] Another aspect is the denial of medical care often occurs with regard to reproductive and sexual health. Sometimes women themselves avoid the medical system for fear of being reported to the police or facing family violence due to having [premarital sex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premarital_sex) or being the victims of [sexual violence](731637.md).[^cite_note-221]

## Violence in specific areas

### Physical violence



Slut shaming is a common form of semiotic violence against women in politics.

#### Politics

Violence Against Women in Politics (VAWP) is the act or threat of physical, emotional or psychological violence against female politicians on the basis of their [gender](38076.md), most often with the intent of discouraging the victims and other female politicians from participating in the political process. VAWP has been growing in significance among the fields of gendered political science and feminist political theory studies. The main intent behind creating a separate category that is distinct from Violence Against Women, is to highlight the barriers faced by women who work in politics, or wish to pursue a career in the political realm. VAWP is unique from Violence Against Women in three important ways: victims are targeted because of their gender; the violence itself can be gendered (i.e., sexism, sexual violence); the primary goal is to deter women from participating in politics (including but not limited to voting, running for office, campaigning, etc.).[^cite_note-222] It is also important to distinguish VAWP from [political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence), which is defined by the use or threats of force to reach political ends, and can be experienced by all politicians.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223]

While women's participation in national parliaments has been increasing, rising from 11% in 1995 to 26.5% in 2023, there is still a large disparity between male and female [representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representation_\(politics\)) in governmental politics.[^cite_note-UN_Women-224] Expanding [women's participation in government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_government) is a crucial goal for many countries, as female politicians have proven invaluable with respect to bringing certain issues to the forefront, such as elimination of gender-based violence, parental leave and childcare, pensions, gender-equality laws, electoral reform, and providing fresh perspectives on numerous policy areas that have typically remained a male-dominated realm.[^cite_note-UN_Women-224] In order to increase women's participation in an effective manner, the importance of recognizing the issues related to VAWP and making every effort to provide the necessary resources to victims and condemn any and all hostile behaviour in political institutions cannot be understated. Experiencing VAWP can dissuade women from remaining in politics (and lead to an early exit from their career or from aspiring higher political office. Witnessing women in politics experience VAWP can serve as one of many deterrents for aspirants to run for office and for candidates to continue campaigning.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223]

Acts of [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence) or [harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harassment) are often not deemed to be gendered when they are reported, if they are reported at all. VAWP is often dismissed as "the cost of doing politics" and reporting can be seen as "political suicide", which contributes to the normalization of VAWP.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223] This ambiguity results in a lack of information regarding attacks and makes the issue appear to be relatively commonplace. While it is reported that [women in politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_government) are more often targeted by violence than their male counterparts,[^cite_note-225] the specific cause is often not reported as a gendered crime. This makes it more difficult to pinpoint where the links between gender-specific violence and political violence really are. In many countries, the practice of electoral politics is traditionally considered to be a [masculine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masculinity) domain.[^cite_note-226]

The history of male dominated politics has allowed some male politicians to believe they have a right to participate in politics while women should not, since women's participation is a threat to the social order. Women in positions of power are thus more likely than their male counterparts to receive threats and experience violence. As one professor of sociology, Marie E. Berry, wrote: "as women are trained and encouraged to vie for these seats, they risk taking power (and resources) that male politicians see as rightfully theirs, opening them up to the risk of violence and other efforts to limit the effectiveness of their campaigns."[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228]

48% of [electoral violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence) against women is against supporters, this is most likely the largest percentage as it has the largest amount of the public participating. 9% of electoral violence against women is targeting candidates, while 22% targets female voters. This means that women who are directly acting in politics are likely to face some form of violence, whether physical or emotional.[^cite_note-Bardall-2011-229] Regarding violence against female politicians, younger women and those with [intersecting identities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersectionality), particularly racial and ethnic minorities, are more likely to be targets. Female politicians who outwardly express and act from feminist perspectives are also more likely to be victimized.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223]

##### Sub-types

Gabrielle Bardell's 2011 report "Breaking the mold: Understanding Gender and Electoral Violence" was one of the first documents published that showed examples and figures for how women are intimidated and attacked in politics.[^cite_note-Bardall-2011-229] Since Bardall's report, other scholars have conducted further research on the topic. Notably, Mona Lena Krook's work on VAWP introduced 5 forms of violence and harassment: physical, sexual, psychological, economic, and semiotic/symbolic. Physical violence encompasses inflicting, or attempting to inflict, bodily harm and injury.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223][^cite_note-Journal_of_Democracy-230] While physical violence is the most easily identified form, it is actually the least common type.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223] Sexual violence involves (attempts at) sexual acts through coercion, including unwanted sexual comments, advances, and harassment.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223][^cite_note-Journal_of_Democracy-230] Psychological violence includes causing emotional and mental damage through means of death/[rape threats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_threats), [stalking](25272420.md), etc.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223][^cite_note-Journal_of_Democracy-230]

Economic violence involves denying, withholding, and controlling female politicians' access to financial resources, particularly regarding campaigns.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223][^cite_note-Journal_of_Democracy-230] Semiotic or symbolic violence, the most abstract subtype of VAWP, refers to the erasure of female politicians through degrading images and sexist language.[^cite_note-Krook-2020-223][^cite_note-Journal_of_Democracy-230][^cite_note-Krook-2022-231] Krook theorizes that semiotic violence against women in politics works in two related ways: rendering women invisible and rendering women incompetent. By symbolically removing women from the public political sphere, semiotic violence renders women invisible. Examples include using masculine grammar when speaking about and to political women, interrupting female politicians, and not portraying political women in the media. By highlighting the role incongruity between stereotypically feminine attributes (e.g., warm, polite, submissive), and traits typically ascribed to good leaders (e.g., strong, powerful, assertive), semiotic violence emphasizes that women are incompetent to be political actors.[^cite_note-Krook-2022-231] This form of semiotic violence can manifest through denying and minimizing women's political qualifications, sexual objectification, and labeling political women as emotional, among other actions.[^cite_note-Krook-2022-231]

#### Higher education

Further information: [Campus sexual assault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campus_sexual_assault)
